Like, I can still have moments where something is fine or mildly nice… if that counts?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lack of energy or fatigue almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, pretty much most days. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I do get things done, but by the afternoon I’m usually really wiped out. And getting myself to soccer after work can feel like that too. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like soccer or anything else, don’t bring you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they don’t bring me pleasure anymore. Like with soccer, I still usually feel like it’s a good thing that I went, and sometimes I enjoy parts of it. Maybe it’s not always as satisfying as I expect it to be, but I don’t know if that’s enough to call it a loss of pleasure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or empty, even if it’s not severe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still go to work and talk to people and everything, but a lot of days there’s this kind of flat, heavy feeling. Like even if work is going okay or people are being nice, I still feel down or sort of empty underneath. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—even if it's not every night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah. I guess I’m here because I’ve just been feeling kind of low for a long time. It kind of started after this dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight. I mean, I still managed and everything, so I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but… yeah, it’s been going on that long.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any days where your mood was noticeably better, where you felt more energetic or even unusually upbeat, even if just for a short time?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so. I mean, I have days where I can get through things a little easier, or I’m distracted and seem more okay, but I wouldn’t say I feel unusually upbeat or energetic. Not in a way that stands out to me, at least. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in doing them altogether?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve lost interest altogether. I still do things I used to like, at least sometimes. Maybe they don’t always feel as enjoyable as they should, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Like, I can still have moments where something is fine or mildly nice… if that counts?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lack of energy or fatigue almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, pretty much most days. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I do get things done, but by the afternoon I’m usually really wiped out. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like soccer or anything else, don’t bring you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they don’t bring me pleasure anymore. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or empty, even if it’s not severe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair. I mean, I have days where I can get through things a little easier, or I’m distracted and seem more okay, but I wouldn’t say I feel unusually upbeat or energetic. Not in a way that stands out to me, at least. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you don’t take as much pleasure in things you used to enjoy, or that you’ve lost interest in doing them altogether?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I don’t think I’ve lost interest altogether. I still do things I used to like, at least sometimes. Maybe they don’t always feel as enjoyable as they should, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Like, I can still have moments where something is fine or mildly nice… if that counts?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lack of energy or fatigue almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, pretty much most days. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I do get things done, but by the afternoon I’m usually really wiped out. Even something small, like fixing a spreadsheet error or finishing expense paperwork, feels way harder than it should—like I’m dragging my brain through mud. And getting myself to soccer after work can feel like that too. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like soccer or anything else, don’t bring you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they don’t bring me pleasure anymore. Like with soccer, I still usually feel like it’s a good thing that I went, and sometimes I enjoy parts of it. Maybe it’s not always as satisfying as I expect it to be, but I don’t know if that’s enough to call it a loss of pleasure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or empty, even if it’s not severe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still go to work and talk to people and everything, but a lot of days there’s this kind of flat, heavy feeling. Like even if work is going okay or people are being nice, I still feel down or sort of empty underneath. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. My appetite seems mostly the same to me. I mean, some days I might eat a little less if I’m busy or not paying attention, but I haven’t noticed any real change or, like, my weight shifting without trying. At least not that I’m aware of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—even if it's not every night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal nights where it takes me a bit to fall asleep if my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a real pattern of insomnia or sleeping way more than usual. My sleep seems… mostly okay, I guess. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you feel restless and can't sit still more often than before?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. I’m probably not the most energetic person, but I wouldn’t say I’m moving slowly in a way other people would notice, and I don’t really feel like I can’t sit still or anything. At least, nothing that stands out to me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have there been any periods lasting a couple of months or more where you felt significantly better—like your mood was clearly improved and the heavy or flat feeling was gone?\n\nPatient: I guess… no, not really for that long. It’s been kind of there since that dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight… so, about two years and two months now, I think. I’ve still been getting through things, even if it hasn’t felt great.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I answering that too vaguely?\n\nDoctor: Have there been any times—maybe lasting a couple of weeks or more—where your low mood got noticeably worse, and you started experiencing other symptoms like feeling hopeless, having trouble concentrating, or even thinking about death or not wanting to go on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it got suddenly worse for a specific couple weeks, exactly. I don’t know if that counts as hopelessness or if I’m being dramatic, but that’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was so low that you felt like you couldn’t go on, or that life wasn’t worth living, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a time where I truly felt like I couldn’t go on, no. I mean… I’ve felt discouraged, and I’ve had thoughts like “what’s the point of trying so hard,” but not in the sense of wanting my life to be over.\n\nI’m sorry if that’s not a clean answer. I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy or feel good?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “no longer,” exactly. Like, I can still have moments where something is fine or mildly nice… if that counts?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lack of energy or fatigue almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, pretty much most days. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I do get things done, but by the afternoon I’m usually really wiped out. Even something small, like fixing a spreadsheet error or finishing expense paperwork, feels way harder than it should—like I’m dragging my brain through mud. And getting myself to soccer after work can feel like that too. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like soccer or anything else, don’t bring you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they don’t bring me pleasure anymore. Like with soccer, I still usually feel like it’s a good thing that I went, and sometimes I enjoy parts of it. Maybe it’s not always as satisfying as I expect it to be, but I don’t know if that’s enough to call it a loss of pleasure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or empty, even if it’s not severe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still go to work and talk to people and everything, but a lot of days there’s this kind of flat, heavy feeling. Like even if work is going okay or people are being nice, I still feel down or sort of empty underneath. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. My appetite seems mostly the same to me. I mean, some days I might eat a little less if I’m busy or not paying attention, but I haven’t noticed any real change or, like, my weight shifting without trying. At least not that I’m aware of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—even if it's not every night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal nights where it takes me a bit to fall asleep if my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a real pattern of insomnia or sleeping way more than usual. My sleep seems… mostly okay, I guess. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you feel restless and can't sit still more often than before?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. I’m probably not the most energetic person, but I wouldn’t say I’m moving slowly in a way other people would notice, and I don’t really feel like I can’t sit still or anything. At least, nothing that stands out to me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have there been any periods lasting a couple of months or more where you felt significantly better—like your mood was clearly improved and the heavy or flat feeling was gone?\n\nPatient: I guess… no, not really for that long. There might’ve been better weeks here and there, but I can’t think of a couple months where it really lifted. It’s been kind of there since that dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight… so, about two years and two months now, I think. I don’t know if that’s the kind of timeline you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time—lasting at least a couple of weeks—where you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in most things to the point that it was really hard to function, even more than you already described?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, at least. I’ve had times where I felt pretty low or kind of checked out, but I don’t know that there was a separate couple-week stretch where it suddenly got much worse and I couldn’t function. I’ve still been getting through things, even if it hasn’t felt great.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I answering that too vaguely?\n\nDoctor: Have there been any times—maybe lasting a couple of weeks or more—where your low mood got noticeably worse, and you started experiencing other symptoms like feeling hopeless, having trouble concentrating, or even thinking about death or not wanting to go on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it got suddenly worse for a specific couple weeks, exactly. But the hopeless part… maybe that fits more than I want to admit.\n\nI used to be really detailed about planning things out, like MBA programs and what steps I’d take career-wise. Lately when I look at those same plans, I kind of just assume I’m not going to get there, no matter how much effort I put in. I don’t know if that counts as hopelessness or if I’m being dramatic, but that’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period where your mood was so low that you felt like you couldn’t go on, or that life wasn’t worth living, even if only for a short time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’ve had a time where I truly felt like I couldn’t go on, no. I mean… I’ve felt discouraged, and I’ve had thoughts like “what’s the point of trying so hard,” but not in the sense of wanting my life to be over.\n\nI’m sorry if that’s not a clean answer. I just don’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that you no longer take pleasure in activities you used to enjoy, even things that used to make you happy or feel good?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “no longer,” exactly. I still can enjoy some things once I’m doing them, but it feels less automatic, I guess. Like I don’t look forward to things as much as I probably used to.\n\nBut I don’t want to make it sound like nothing makes me happy at all, because that’s not true. It’s more… muted, maybe? Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Would you say there have been times when even the things you used to really enjoy, like soccer or spending time with friends, felt completely flat or didn’t bring you any real pleasure at all—even for a few weeks or more?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say completely flat for weeks, no. Like with soccer or seeing friends, sometimes I’ll drag my feet getting myself to go, and it might not feel as exciting as it used to… but once I’m there, I can still have moments where it feels good.\n\nSo, I guess my answer is no—not in that total “nothing feels enjoyable” way. I don’t think I’ve lost interest altogether. I still do things I used to like, at least sometimes. Maybe they don’t always feel as enjoyable as they should, but I wouldn’t want to overstate it. Like, I can still have moments where something is fine or mildly nice… if that counts?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling a lack of energy or fatigue almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I have, pretty much most days. I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t function, because I do get things done, but by the afternoon I’m usually really wiped out. Even something small, like fixing a spreadsheet error or finishing expense paperwork, feels way harder than it should—like I’m dragging my brain through mud. And getting myself to soccer after work can feel like that too. Is that the kind of fatigue you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you used to enjoy, like soccer or anything else, don’t bring you the same sense of pleasure or satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they don’t bring me pleasure anymore. Like with soccer, I still usually feel like it’s a good thing that I went, and sometimes I enjoy parts of it. Maybe it’s not always as satisfying as I expect it to be, but I don’t know if that’s enough to call it a loss of pleasure. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Would you say that on most days, you feel down or empty, even if it’s not severe?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably fair. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I can still go to work and talk to people and everything, but a lot of days there’s this kind of flat, heavy feeling. Like even if work is going okay or people are being nice, I still feel down or sort of empty underneath. That’s actually a big part of why I came in, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your appetite or weight over this time—like eating more or less than usual, or gaining or losing weight without trying?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. My appetite seems mostly the same to me. I mean, some days I might eat a little less if I’m busy or not paying attention, but I haven’t noticed any real change or, like, my weight shifting without trying. At least not that I’m aware of. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep—like trouble falling asleep, staying asleep, or sleeping much more than usual—even if it's not every night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a major way. I mean, I have normal nights where it takes me a bit to fall asleep if my mind is busy, but I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed a real pattern of insomnia or sleeping way more than usual. My sleep seems… mostly okay, I guess. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you're moving more slowly than usual, or that you feel restless and can't sit still more often than before?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think I’ve noticed that, no. I’m probably not the most energetic person, but I wouldn’t say I’m moving slowly in a way other people would notice, and I don’t really feel like I can’t sit still or anything. At least, nothing that stands out to me. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Over the past two years, have there been any periods lasting a couple of months or more where you felt significantly better—like your mood was clearly improved and the heavy or flat feeling was gone?\n\nPatient: I guess… no, not really for that long. There might’ve been better weeks here and there, but I can’t think of a couple months where it really lifted. It’s been kind of there since that dashboard project at work put me more in the spotlight… so, about two years and two months now, I think. I don’t know if that’s the kind of timeline you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time—lasting at least a couple of weeks—where you felt so down, hopeless, or lost interest in most things to the point that it was really hard to function, even more than you already described?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like that, at least. I’ve had times where I felt pretty low or kind of checked out, but I don’t know that there was a separate couple-week stretch where it suddenly got much worse and I couldn’t function. I’ve still been getting through things, even if it hasn’t felt great.\n\nDoes that make sense, or am I answering that too vaguely?\n\nDoctor: Have there been any times—maybe lasting a couple of weeks or more—where your low mood got noticeably worse, and you started experiencing other symptoms like feeling hopeless, having trouble concentrating, or even thinking about death or not wanting to go on?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it got suddenly worse for a specific couple weeks, exactly. I mean… I’ve felt discouraged, and I’ve had thoughts like “what’s the point of trying so hard,” but not in the sense of wanting my life to be over.\n\nI’m sorry if that’s not a clean answer. Like I don’t look forward to things as much as I probably used to.\n\nBut I don’t want to make it sound like nothing makes me happy at all, because that’s not true. It’s more… muted, maybe?
